# M0 cross-item fix queue — the one routing surface (N-53 (7), J243)

Every defect one item reports against ANOTHER item (or "for the session", "for X's fix round", "the session routes")
gets a row HERE the moment it is seen — never only a sentence in a choices or notes row (that is how C-0177 (10) sat
unrouted for four days). The session works this queue top-down as seats free; a row leaves only with a commit hash or a
ruling id. M0 is the bedrock (J238): nothing here is waived; the M0 hardening phase (J237) drains whatever is left.

Status: **OPEN** (not started) · **RULING** (a ruling seat is deciding) · **ROUND** (a fix round is running) ·
**DONE <hash>** · **HARDENING** (parked for the M0 hardening phase with a reason).

THE ANSWER TO THIS ROW'S QUESTION: THE STAGE ALONE IS NOT WITHIN TWO ORDERS OF MAGNITUDE OF THE BAR — the loaded box's 223 s mean was the stage's own cost, not load or the bench's measurement (the idle box is faster on S3 / S4 / S5 and slower on S8: seeds 1–3 against the 40-record mean, a seed effect of ≈ 10 %); ≈ 200 ms a cut at ≈ 1 194 cuts a seed against §12.4's 12 µs (KI-12's 135–138 ms a cut was the seeded tree's contended figure); no reference-machine normalisation closes 10^3 (a Mac is a few × a 2.8 GHz Xeon single-threaded, not 10^3), and §4.4's pre-decided fallback (growth stops at district-scale secondaries everywhere; lanes infill-only) does not touch S8's cost — S8 IS the infill, and the fallback hands it more of the fabric — so the fallback cannot carry the bar on its own: the decision the owner placed after the Mac steps (J558 (2)) is between an S8 whose per-cut cost comes down by ≈ 10^3–10^4 (an implementation term, as Q18's scratch arena was) and a bar re-declared by three orders. | Q121 | THE FURNISHING CATALOGUE AND THE REACH LAW — a base change request to V03 (the interiors volume; its §2.4 catalogue and §3.8 F1–F4), from the owner's design conversation of 2026-10-28 05:3x–05:5xZ. (1) THREE TIERS per room role: essential (a bedroom's beds equal its occupants, a chapel's altar, a smithy's anvil — counted from the room's function), optional (by weight, per class and band) and DECOR (by density; never on circulation) — in place of today's single weighted list of 24 glyphs; the catalogue grows to the order of two hundred semantic glyphs across the room roles, authored with the roster so that a master smith's shop front and a lean-to forge differ in objects, not only in rooms. (2) THE SEMANTIC-GLYPH LAW: the document stores the semantic glyph (`bed`, `loom`), its position, rotation and size — never an art asset; every style (ink, parchment, a colour battle-map preset) is the renderer's mapping of glyph → drawing, so a style never shifts a document or a golden (the §17 clean-room posture: the art is ours, drawn or procedural). (3) THE DECOR LAYER derived, not stored: indoor clutter per room from the room's stream on §8.10's decoration-instance pattern (scatter is derived and unstored), respecting F2's free floor, becoming a plan value only when a user edits one (the §2.9 document-size test stays at 320 B per building). (4) THE REACH LAW (THE OWNER, 05:5xZ: 'make sure that when you are spreading out these objects … there is at least space to walk and reach everything with an arm reach. Some exceptions may apply like a storage room has objects that are in front of other objects that are not reachable without removing said objects in the front'): the WALKABLE REGION of a room is the free floor of F2 connected to the room's entry door (the walkway band is its spine, never its whole); an item is REACHABLE when its clear side lies within arm's reach (≈ 70 cm, a calibratable cell) of the walkable region; F3's fit test admits an item only if it is reachable AND leaves the walkable region connected to every door and every head; a room role carries `reach: 'all' | 'front'` — `front` for the storage roles (store, cellar, attic, warehouse, a loft) where stacked rows are lawful and only the front row must be reachable, and a `stack` placement puts items behind others there; a placement that would leave an item unreachable in an `all` room is skipped and counted in the report. (5) THE PLANES (THE OWNER, 05:5xZ: 'an object stacked on top of another object or an object hanging over another object like a desk with a lamp … or a table that's underneath a picture or mirror'): every glyph carries a `plane` — `floor` (occupies floor: today's catalogue), `surface` (sits on a host item's top: a lamp, an inkwell, books, a candlestick, a bowl, a loaf — each row names its hosts (`on: ['desk','table','chest','counter','shelf', …]`) and a host row its capacity (`hosts: n`); a surface item occupies no floor and is reachable through its host), `wall` (hung on a wall at a height band: a picture, a mirror, a sconce, a shelf, a rack, hooks, a crucifix — a wall slot above a floor item is lawful when that item is below the hanging height, so a mirror hangs over a table and a shelf over a bench, never behind a `tall` item; a wall item occupies no floor; reachable when its band is at or below ≈ 180 cm, decorative above) and `ceiling` (a hanging lamp, a drying rack, a chandelier — placed over free floor or over a `surface`-capable host, never over a head or a stair); VERTICAL STACKS: a `stackable` floor glyph (crate, barrel, sack, bale) may stand on its own kind up to `stackMax`, freely in `front` rooms and up to reach height (≈ 180 cm) in `all` rooms — the horizontal rows-in-front exception of (4) and the vertical stack are the two storage forms; every plane is placed by the same F3 pass after the floor items, in order floor → surface → wall → ceiling, by the same weights and caps, and drawn at the treads stop as small marks on or over their host (the ink preset's own marks; a VTT scene's image only). (6) The bars: T-V3-8 gains the reach clause (every floor item reachable in an `all` room; the walkable region connected door to door and to every head; rows in front only in `front` roles) and the plane clauses (every surface item inside its host's top ring and within its capacity; every wall item on a wall segment with no `tall` floor item before it and within its height band; no ceiling item over a head or a stair; stacks within `stackMax` and, in `all` rooms, within reach height); T-V3-35's calibration adds the walkable share of the floor per role and the surface-and-wall item counts per role against reference plans. (7) THE SESSION'S OWN ADDITIONS (vetoable; from the comprehensive review of 05:46Z): (a) ARRANGEMENTS — a `group` row type: a composed cluster of glyphs with relative placements and tolerances (a table with two to six seats around it; a bed with a chest at its foot; a desk with its seat and a lamp; a forge cluster — hearth, anvil, bellows, quench trough; a loom with its bench), placed by F3 as ONE footprint by the group's weight and then owned as individual plan values (editable one by one, the `instance` event with a group ordinal for a move of the whole) — the single largest quality lever: real rooms are clusters, not independent items placed largest-first; (b) AFFORDANCES — a `faces` column (wall / room / host / window / hearth) and a `near` affinity (window, hearth, door, a host glyph) per glyph, read by F3's slot choice: a bed's head to the wall, a desk toward the window, seats toward their table, a settle toward the hearth, a sconce by the door; (c) THE CALIBRATION SOURCE for the tiers' counts and densities: historical probate inventories (public-domain, room-by-room object lists of real houses by wealth and trade, 16th–18th c.) as the reference corpus of T-V3-35's furnishing clauses, beside the reference plans — a curated few dozen rooms per band and trade, kept under the clean-room rule as data, never drawings; (d) CULTURE AND ERA as keys: the role lists, the group rows and the decor pools keyed by the same culture and era rows the naming grammar and the roster read, so another culture furnishes differently without a schema change; (e) the standing principle the whole request serves: PLAT's advantage over an asset-library tool is coherence — the town explains the house and the house explains the room — so the essentials come from the occupants and the trade, the clusters from function, the styles from the renderer, and breadth is a content job done later and cheaply. (8) VARIANCE AND WEAR (THE OWNER, 06:12Z: 'variance within generated rooms is also important to keep it from looking stale … random effects where appropriate, like an article of clothing chosen at random from several on the floor of the bedroom unfolded. Or rugs. Tiny little discrepancies like this that make it feel more lived in. Or an apartment complex that generally has the same furniture, but the layout is different. Some optimal, some are not. Or the quality of the furniture is different, coinciding with how we have different material for building … so too should the furniture. Maybe the furniture is aged or damaged from what we can reasonably see'): every such effect is a DRAW from the room's stream (§6.1; the `plan.furnish` concern, stable per building under the elaboration record — item 28 (b): generated once, kept until something changes; `New random` re-rolls on request), never stored until a user edits the item, so the variance is deterministic, reproducible and free of document growth. (a) LAYOUT VARIANCE: F3's scan start among the admissible corners, each group's orientation among its lawful ones and the slot order are drawn, so identical programmes in identical shells (an apartment block, a terrace, a barracks) lay out differently; a per-household TIDINESS draw (from the band's range) admits a second-best lawful slot with a small probability — 'some optimal, some not' — never a slot that breaks the reach law or the planes (untidy means suboptimal, never unlawful). (b) LIVED-IN TOUCHES: a decor pool per role with low-weight, draw-chosen items — a garment unfolded on a bedroom floor (one of several), a rug, a dropped tool, a bowl left out — at the band's decor density, on the floor or a host, never on circulation. (c) QUALITY AND MATERIAL as item attributes, not glyphs: `grade` (drawn within the household's band, jittered per household) and `material` (from the same regional material rows V02's building materials read — oak, pine, wicker, iron), carried on the item and drawn by the renderer as marks and tones; a glyph stays `bed`. (d) WEAR AND DAMAGE: a per-item `wear` on the plan's damage-layer pattern (item 123: one keyed threshold per element, so the same chair breaks first and stays broken) derived from the building's condition and age (V06's decay runs), the household's band and the item's grade — drawn at the treads stop as a cracked top, a missing leg, a sagging bed — and the ruin ladder's 'the furniture breaks' becomes this field's own progression. (e) THE BARS: T-V3-8 gains 'variance never breaks (4)–(5)'; a DISPERSION bar in T-V3-35's form — units with identical programmes in identical shells differ in layout above a floor (the between-seed dispersion idea of §16's M1 bars, applied between units), and the untidy share per band within its range; the wear distribution per building condition against the damage fixtures (T-V3-39's levels) (9) THE VARIATION AXES (THE OWNER, 2026-10-30 13:4xZ, J634: 'multiple variations of each glyph, depending on both layout, style, building material, orientation — as in whether it is diagonal, toppled over, upside down or even broken or shattered — whether it is on the wall or on the ground or on top of or underneath another object stacked, each appropriate for prosperity bands, institution and room type'): a glyph stays ONE semantic row (`chair`); its variation is attributes and draws, never new glyphs and never art in the model — `material` and `grade` ((8) (c)); `style` (the renderer's mapping by the culture and era keys of (7) (d)); a drawn `variant` ordinal (the renderer's atlas holds several drawn forms per glyph and material class, procedural or parametric where it can, so two chairs in one room differ); `plane` ((5)); `pose` (upright / leaning / toppled / overturned — a state drawn from the room's stream, gated by (11)); `condition` on the wear ladder of (8) (d) extended by `broken` and `shattered` (a shattered item spawns debris as decor-layer instances on the free floor — unstored, walk-over for the reach test, drawn as shards); and a per-row `rotFree` flag (a decor-tier or lived-in item may lie at a drawn angle — the owner's 'diagonal' — while an essential keeps its `faces` affordance and a seat its group's angle tolerance); every axis is derived from the room's stream and stored only when a user edits the item (the §2.9 320 B test holds). (10) THE COUNT LAWS AND THE APPROPRIATENESS KEYS (THE OWNER: 'tables with chairs equal to or just about or slightly more than the number of occupants where appropriate; a number of baskets for a common shared kitchen; multiple stands or styles for a larger shop; weapons and armor and tools and trinkets of various kinds for a blacksmith shop scaling to how big their operation is'): the essential tier's counts come from facts the town already holds, as a `CountLawRow` per (room role, institution or trade) — seats = occupants + a drawn [0, 2] where the role dines or meets, a `substitutes` column on the glyph row standing in below a band (bench ↔ chairs, pallet ↔ bed, chest ↔ wardrobe); beds = occupants by the household's sleeping rule; the vessels, baskets and pots of a shared kitchen ∝ the households that share it; a shop's counters, stands and displays ∝ its SIZE tier, each a distinct variant; a workshop's wares (a smithy's blades, helms, tools and trinkets on racks, hooks and the counter) a trade-keyed pool scaled by the operation's tier from the host's dossier (V08) or the class ladder (V11 §3.5); every list is keyed (role × institution or trade × band × culture and era), the band as §2.4's four-column cell. (11) THE COHERENCE PROGRAMME (THE OWNER: 'an appropriate coherence program to make sure that it all looks right and there is still a bit of randomness variation to all of this — articles of clothing on the ground or a toppled over chair, for example'): one named component of `plan.furnish` with a GENERATOR side and a CHECKER side. The generator applies the count laws; one material family and grade range per household with per-item jitter inside it; one style key per institution; the CONTEXT GATES on pose and condition — toppled and overturned only within the household's untidy share, in a storage role, in a vacant, abandoned or ruined plan (V06's states) or after a disaster in the damage layer (§5.6); broken and shattered only from the wear ladder's keyed thresholds; never in a chancel or a rich hall at full tidiness — the groups and affordances of (7), and a bounded RANDOMNESS BUDGET (the tidiness, lived-in, pose and variant draws of (8) inside the laws' tolerances: untidy means suboptimal or lived-in, never unlawful or incoherent). The checker is the bars: T-V3-8 gains the coherence clauses (every essential count within its law's tolerance; one material family per household; the gates respected; (4)–(5) never broken by a variation), T-V3-35's calibration gains the counts per role and tier against the probate corpus of (7) (c) and the reference plans, and the dispersion bar of (8) (e) stays, so coherence never becomes sameness; the clerk's room 'why' (§4.6) names the law behind each essential. (12) THE SESSION'S READING (vetoable, J634): the laws are the product and the variants are content — (7) (e)'s principle; the new attributes join the DOCUMENT-SCHEMA seam that settles before the first golden (J567; the shape before M2a), the rows land at M6-P; rejected: variants as separate glyphs (the catalogue would multiply by material × style × pose and the document would carry art decisions), storing poses and variants for every item (growth; the draw is reproducible), free rotation for essentials (the faces affordance is what makes a room read as a room). (13) THE BANDS (THE OWNER, 2026-10-30 13:5xZ, J635: 'Untidy should be a band. Most of these should be a band. That has an effect weighted, random roll and ceiling and floor for each glyph'): the qualities of (8)–(11) are DISCRETE AUTHORED BANDS on the prosperity band's own pattern (§2.4's four-column cells, §4.5's discrete-cell rule — the nearest rowed rung below), not hidden scalars: a TIDINESS band (immaculate / tidy / lived-in / untidy / disordered), an UPKEEP band modulating the wear ladder of (8) (d) (kept / sound / worn / neglected / derelict; the building's condition and V06's decay set its ceiling), a FULLNESS band for the optional and decor tiers (bare / sparse / furnished / full / cluttered) and a GRADE band for quality and material (8) (c) — each a `BandRow` per rung that gives, per glyph tier with per-glyph overrides, a FLOOR and a CEILING (a count or a share for the essential and optional tiers — the count law of (10) fixes the essential's centre and the rung widens or shifts its tolerance, a squalid rung's floor below the occupants, a rich rung's surplus above — a density for the decor tier, a probability for each pose and condition state) and the WEIGHTS of the roll between them (an authored mode, so 'weighted' is content, not code); the roll is one draw from the room's stream between floor and ceiling (stable per building, re-rolled by New random, stored only when edited). THE RUNG ASSIGNMENT is itself a weighted draw per household or institution keyed by the prosperity band, the culture and era rows and the institution's type (a rich household is likelier tidy and may still draw untidy — 'some optimal, some not'; a barracks and a tavern draw their tidiness differently), so the dispersion of (8) (e) arises from the rung draws; a user may SET a rung as a plan edit (`plan.band.*`, one plan value, the clerk names it in the room why). The coherence programme's gates of (11) become band-conditioned ceilings (a toppled chair's probability per tidiness rung; shattered's ceiling 0 in the top upkeep rungs and in a chancel); T-V3-35's calibration cell is per rung (the probate corpus binned by its own tidiness and upkeep readings where the inventories tell them). KEPT SMALL (the session's reading, vetoable): four bands of at most five rungs; floors and ceilings authored per tier with glyph overrides, never a full matrix per glyph; the prosperity band stays the master key and the others are drawn conditioned on it; every band's rows carry provenance and the calibration register like §2.4's. (14) THE SCALE AXIS (THE OWNER, 2026-10-31 00:3xZ, J654: 'Some of the glyphs should scale by the size of the room and prosperity — think of a common area: if it's a more prosperous area, rather than several tables it's just one bigger, larger table. The chair sizes of course have to be standard, and scaled appropriately in terms of numbers to the table. Think of a huge forge: maybe there's one giant anvil somewhere, or a giant forge. It doesn't change the shape of the glyph, it changes the proportions'): every glyph row carries a `scale` law — `fixed` (the human-scaled items: a chair, a stool, a bed, a tool in the hand — one standard size from the anthropometric cells, never scaled; grade and material jitter its marks, not its footprint; its COUNT answers the room by (10)), `length` (one axis scales, the other stays standard: a bench, a counter, a shelf, a rack, a trough, a pew — a bench grows along its seats at the standard seat pitch) and `area` (the footprint scales: a table, a hearth, a forge, an anvil, a vat, a kiln, an altar, a display stand); a `length` or `area` glyph's size is drawn between its row's [min, max] by a SIZE LAW keyed on what the town already knows — a table by the seats the count law of (10) asks for (seats = occupants + the drawn surplus; the table's capacity = its perimeter ÷ the seat pitch, ≈ 60–70 cm, a calibratable cell), a forge and its anvil by the operation's tier from the host's dossier (V08) or the class ladder (V11 §3.5), a hearth by the room's area and the household's band — and never beyond what the reach law of (4) leaves walkable: scaling never breaks (4) or (5). THE ANCHOR AND THE DIRECTION OF WEALTH (THE OWNER'S CORRECTION, 2026-10-31 — after 00:36Z, recorded 01:23Z — J656: 'Rich places have more stuff and also larger proportions to what the centerpiece of the room is. Poor people have less stuff that just happens to also be smaller cause I can't afford much and also I have limited space'): every room role names its ANCHOR glyph — the centrepiece the room is about (the hall's table, the smithy's forge and anvil, the kitchen's hearth, the chapel's altar, the shop's counter, the tavern's bar, the bedroom's bed) — and wealth moves everything in ONE direction: a rich room has MORE things (the fullness band of (13) at full or cluttered — more optional and decor items, more seats, more on every host) AND a LARGER anchor (its size law's [min, max] window shifts up with the rung and with the room's area); a poor room has FEWER things (bare or sparse) AND a SMALLER anchor (the window shifts down — 'can't afford much' — and is capped by the room's area — 'limited space'); no band makes a poor room many-and-small. CONSOLIDATION is a rule about the anchor's FORM only: where the anchor's required capacity would take several ordinary units, the rich rungs lay ONE great piece (one long table seating twenty; one great forge and anvil for the master smith), the modest rungs several ordinary pieces (five tables for twenty), and the poor rungs seldom meet the choice (a small capacity is one small piece) and take (10)'s substitutes where they do (a trestle and benches); the preference is a `consolidate` weight per prosperity rung in (13)'s `BandRow`, GATED by the role's count-law row of (10): the role says whether its anchor may consolidate at all (a hall, a refectory, a chapter house: yes; a tavern, a schoolroom, a scriptorium: no — their many tables or desks are the function, however rich, and a rich tavern has more and better tables and a longer bar); a consolidated host's `hosts` capacity (5) grows with its top. THE CHAIRS: a seat glyph's count law reads its host's capacity (`seatsOf(table)`), so chairs follow the table — standard in size, scaled in number — and a group row of (7) (a) carries the pairing (a table with its seats around it at the pitch) so a long table seats a long row. PROPORTION, NOT SHAPE (the owner's law): the semantic glyph stays one row; the document already stores an item's size (Q121 (2): glyph, position, rotation, size), so the axis adds no schema — the renderer draws the glyph parametrically in its size (a table stretched along its axis with its ends kept, an anvil scaled uniformly, a bench by its seats), the atlas holding size classes only where a parametric draw cannot. THE BARS: T-V3-8 gains the scale clauses — a `fixed` glyph at its standard size (the grade and material marks aside); a `length` or `area` glyph within its row's [min, max] and inside (4)'s walkable region; a consolidation preserving capacity within the count law's tolerance; seats per table equal to the table's capacity within that tolerance; T-V3-35's calibration adds table length per seat against the reference plans (the refectory and hall long tables, the cottage board) and forge and anvil size per operation tier against the craft inventories of (7) (c). KEPT SMALL (the session's reading, vetoable): three values of `scale`, one `anchor` glyph per role row, the size laws authored per row with the count laws of (10), consolidation a band weight under the role's gate and never a new glyph; rejected: a glyph per size class (contradicts (9) — one semantic row), scaling the human-scaled items (a chair must fit a person), free scaling (bounded by the row and by (4)). (15) COLLECTIONS, TROPHIES AND THE ECLECTIC RICH (THE OWNER, 2026-10-31, recorded 01:27Z, J658: 'Back then, rich people were not minimalists. They were quite the eclectic collectors. Speaking of which also should be like animal heads as well, trophies, and collectibles or mounted stuffed heads in the likes'): the MORE THINGS of (14)'s rich room are COLLECTIONS — the fullness band of (13) at its rich rungs is full or cluttered by a `collection` pool, never by duplicates of the essentials: a tier-pool like decor, keyed (role × institution × band × culture and era) as (10)'s lists are — WALL plane ((5)): mounted heads and antlers (stag, boar, bear, wolf), horns, hides, shields and crossed weapons, banners, tapestries, paintings and portraits, maps, mirrors, clocks; SURFACE plane: curios — relics and reliquaries, specimens in jars, shells, coins and medals, instruments (an astrolabe, a globe), books and scrolls, figurines, plate and goblets on a sideboard; FLOOR plane: a suit of armour on its stand, a bust or statue on a plinth, a cabinet of curiosities, a display case, a standing stuffed beast, a globe stand, a lectern with a great book; CEILING: a hung banner, a ship model, an antler chandelier. THE GLYPH LAW HOLDS ((2), (9)): `mounted_head` is one semantic glyph with `species` an attribute, `painting` one glyph with `subject` an attribute — never a glyph per species or subject; the atlas draws the marks at the treads stop; collections are derived like decor ((3)), stored only when a user edits one. THE ECLECTIC LAW: the pool is drawn for VARIETY — kinds sampled without replacement up to the room's collection count, repeats lawful only inside a declared `set` group of (7) (a) (a row of portraits, a wall of heads, a shelf of jars) — so a rich hall reads as a collector's room, not a warehouse of one thing; the collection count per room is the fullness rung's floor and ceiling for this pool (high at the rich rungs; zero or ONE heirloom at the poor rungs — a poor room's single treasured object is lawful and telling). COHERENCE OF A COLLECTION ((11)): one or two THEMES per household (three at the top rungs) drawn from its dossier, trade and history — the hunter's or lord's heads and hides; the voyager's shells, maps and foreign curios; the scholar's or physician's instruments, specimens and books; the pious household's relics; the soldier's arms and banners; the merchant's plate — so eclectic means many kinds inside a few themes, never noise; the grade band sets quality (a gilt frame or a plain one), the upkeep band and (8) (d) the wear (a moth-eaten head, a cracked jar); V06's events (a hunt, a campaign, a voyage, an inheritance) may name a trophy's provenance in the clerk's room why (§4.6) — content, not schema. THE GATES ((11)): heads and trophies in halls, lodges, studies, taverns and a hunter's or lord's house; relics in chapels, vestries and pious houses; instruments, specimens, maps and books in studies, a scholar's or physician's house, a guild hall; arms and armour in a hall, a barracks, a lord's house; plate on a rich sideboard; never a head in a chancel or a nursery; culture and era keyed (a culture that mounts no heads mounts none; a guild's tokens of office differ by guild); the trade dossier (V08) and the class ladder (V11 §3.5) scale a hunter's heads and a merchant's curios with the operation. PLACEMENT: wall pieces in the hanging band over the anchor and the seats ((5)); floor pieces along walls and in corners, never on circulation, the reach law (4) holding (a display case or cabinet is a `front`-reach storage form); ceiling pieces over free floor. THE BARS: T-V3-8 gains the collection clauses — the count within the fullness rung's window; variety (outside declared sets, distinct kinds ≥ ⌈n / 2⌉ for n ≥ 4); the gates respected; themes within the household's cap; T-V3-35's calibration adds collection counts and kinds per band and role against the probate inventories of (7) (c) — the great-house inventories list exactly such goods — and the reference plans' halls; the dispersion bar of (8) (e) counts collection kinds between households. KEPT SMALL (the session's reading, vetoable): one `collection` pool beside decor, species and subject as attributes, themes a per-household draw of two or three keys; rejected: a glyph per species or subject, stored collections, unlimited themes. | the owner, 2026-10-28 (the catalogue question 05:4xZ, the reach rule 05:5xZ), 2026-10-30 (the variation axes, the count laws and the coherence programme, J634; the bands, J635) and 2026-10-31 (the scale axis, J654; the anchor and the direction of wealth — the owner's correction, J656; collections and trophies, J658); the session's recommendations (vetoable) 05:4xZ and J634; V03 §2.4, §3.8, §7 | V03's owner at the plan rung's design round: the record shape (`Storey.furniture[]`'s tier field, the glyph key law, the derived decor layer) is a DOCUMENT-SCHEMA seam — settle it before the first golden (M1 / M2a, J567's exit rule) even though the rows land at M6-P; the catalogue rows at M6-P with M-CAL's calibration | OPEN (J592, J634, J635, J654, J656, J658) → V03's next design round (the shape before M2a; the rows at M6-P) |

| Q136 | THE DOMINANT CULTURE OF THE EXTERIOR — a base change request to V02 (the buildings volume: its composition, roof, facade and ornament rows), V11 (the rows) and V08 (the dossier's culture mix), from the owner's design words of 2026-10-31 (J660: 'We should have a dominating culture aspect as well to the outward appearance of building … for each of the cultures, how do you imagine the buildings to subtly change? We should think about that. Think of all the cultures that we currently have this far'; J662: 'I would focus less on the building material because that is going to be a product of the resources that they have at hand and focus more on the shapes and layout, both of buildings, of urban layout and specific glyphs for interior … When a settlement is generated, it is generated homogeneous in culture. As people move around, depending on who is the dominant culture and the ratio and mixing of cultures — like 80 % versus 20 % — new buildings and layout beyond what is currently built, or renovations where reasonable or appropriate, integrate that ratio'). (0) THE FOCUS (J662): SHAPES AND LAYOUT, not materials — the building's form (massing, roof, facade, ornament), the URBAN LAYOUT (streets, blocks, plots, open space, the market) and the INTERIOR GLYPHS; materials stay what they are today, a product of the resources at hand (V11 §3.19's era and region rows and the regional shift), and a culture never selects them. TODAY: the culture key is `site.culture` (V08 §2.6's `cultures` row — `germanic`, `latin` (alias `mediterranean`), `celtic`, `arabic`, `norse`, `slavic`, `east_asian`, `mesoamerican`, `south_asian`, `steppe`, `greek`, `mixed`) and it reaches a building's outside in three places only: V11 §3.24.3's stepped specialisations (tiers for `mesoamerican`, `south_asian`, `east_asian`), DR43's festival decorations (V11's motif rows) and the material rows' regional shift (V11 §3.19, keyed by a REGION preset, not by culture); the massing families, roofs, facades and permanent ornament read class and band only, and materials are texture and colour, never massing (the owner, 2026-09-26; T-V2-25 — kept). (1) HOMOGENEOUS AT CREATION, MIXED BY TIME (J662 — the owner's host-side model): a settlement is GENERATED in one culture, `site.culture`, and every stage reads that one key; the MIX is V06's — each epoch the host (V08's dossier, the owner's side) gives the culture RATIO of the population (`cultureShares`: the dominant and the minorities, 80 : 20 and the like) and V06 integrates it in three ways and no other: (a) NEW BUILDING — a building founded in the epoch (growth, infill, a rebuild on a ruin) draws its culture by the ratio of the households that fill it (S10b's allocation carries the household's culture), so the new fabric carries the mix; (b) NEW LAYOUT beyond what is built — an expansion ring, a new nucleus or a new quarter (V06's `encroach` and `newNucleus` rows) is laid with the LAYOUT CELLS of the culture whose households it is for, so a minority that clusters gets a quarter in its own street form while the built streets persist as they stand (the old plan persists, §4.5's rule); (c) RENOVATION where reasonable — a standing building whose household changes culture, or that reaches a renovation event of V06's decay runs, re-derives its facade, ornament and interior under the new culture's rows while its massing and plot stand until a rebuild (a facade is cheap, a footprint is not); never a retrofit of a street. CLUSTERING: below a `quarterThreshold` share a minority is dispersed households renovating; above it the epoch founds or converts a quarter for it; a minority's own worship or civic building appears above a `signalThreshold` share; the town-level fabric (walls, gates, towers, bridges, the civic core, the market) stays the founding culture's until a rebuild event of its own — so a town reads as its founders' with the layers of its later people, never as a patchwork drawn at birth. (2) THE CULTURE EXTERIOR PROFILE (`CultureExteriorRow`, V11; one per culture key; `mixed` a weighted blend of its mix), read by V02's composition chain AFTER class and band, so footprints, counts and plot laws never change: (a) family weights — a multiplier on the class row's massing-family weights within the class's family set (a courtyard culture raises `compound`, a timber culture raises `row` with jetties), never a family outside the set and never a new footprint law; (b) roof rows — pitch band, form weights (gable, hip, half-hip, flat with parapet, tiered, turf), eaves depth and gable orientation to the street; (c) facade rows — the opening rhythm (window proportion and count per bay, the ground floor's blind share), jetties, oriels, loggias and arcades, balconies and screens, external stairs, the street wall's blankness; (d) MATERIALS — none: the culture selects no material (J662; the resources at hand do, through V11 §3.19's region and era rows); a culture row may carry a colour and finish PALETTE applied to whatever material the region gives (a limewash, a stain, a painted motif), nothing more; (e) permanent ornament — carved boards, brackets, string courses, relief, tilework, finials and ridge beasts, painted motifs — as decoration instances on §8.10's pattern (derived, unstored, drawn at the treads stop), keyed culture × class × band; (f) the signal buildings — each culture's one or two unmistakable silhouettes on its worship and civic classes (a dome, a stave roof, a tented spire, a minaret, a stepped platform, a tiered tower, a loggia front) through V11 §3.24.3's specialisation table widened to every culture; (g) THE URBAN LAYOUT (`CultureLayoutRow`, V11; J662) — the culture's values for the layout stages' DECLARED CONTROL CELLS (§7.8's controls; the laws untouched): S4's road grammar weights (grid, organic, radial; the arterial angle bands), S5's growth cells (block depth, the cul-de-sac share, the compound-wall share), S6's open-space kind (square, green, plaza, maidan, a temple tank) and the market form (open, covered, hall), S8's cut form weights (courtyard blocks against rows; the inward house behind a blank street wall against the street-facing row), S10's plot aspect bands and setbacks, S7's civic-core form (the axial precinct, the piazza, the platform plaza, the citadel and caravanserai); applied to the whole town at creation and, after (1), to new ground only; (h) THE INTERIOR GLYPHS (Q121's catalogue; J662) — the glyph rows gain a `cultures` column and the catalogue gains CULTURE-SPECIFIC GLYPHS where a culture's object is its own and not a style of a shared one (a sleeping platform, a floor-cushion seat, a low table, a brazier, a clay oven, a grinding stone, a prayer niche, a screen, a bathing tub, a loom of another form), each a semantic row under Q121 (2)'s law; a room role's ESSENTIALS are mapped per culture (the role 'sleeping' is a bed here and a platform or a mat there; 'seating' a chair, a bench or a cushion; 'cooking' a hearth, a brazier or an oven), so Q121 (10)'s count laws keyed by culture choose the glyph as well as the count, and shared glyphs take the culture's `style` ((9)) at the renderer. (3) THE SUBTLETY LAW, REVISED (J662 — the layout is the culture's too): every kernel law and every stage LAW is culture-blind; a culture speaks only through DECLARED CELLS and ROWS — the layout cells of (2) (g), the exterior rows of (2) (a)–(f) and the glyph rows of (2) (h) — so the same seed in two cultures gives two towns whose every difference is accounted for by the cells and rows that differ; the bar (T-V2 and T-V11 new): a culture swap with the culture's cells and rows held at the other culture's values changes no digest (the culture is nothing but its cells); the between-culture dispersion bar of §16's M1 kind stands (two cultures' towns of one seed differ above a floor in layout and in buildings; two seeds of one culture stay inside the culture's envelope); 'subtly' means the envelopes overlap — a germanic and a slavic town share most of their fabric and differ in the tells. (4) THE SESSION'S SKETCHES (vetoable; the c14 era row's reading of each vernacular — the era row shifts them; fantasy presets may re-weight; J662: the material words below are illustrative of what those regions had at hand — the rows carry the SHAPES, and (4b) the LAYOUT): `germanic` — timber frame with jetties, steep gables to the street, red tile or slate, stone ground floors, oriels and dormers, painted plaster between carved beams, stepped brick gables in the north; `latin` — rendered masonry in ochre, terracotta and white, low pantile roofs, arcades and loggias along the street, tower-houses in the great towns, shuttered small windows, courtyards, cornices and string courses; `greek` — whitewashed rubble and stone, low tile or flat roofs, domes on worship, courtyards and external stairs, deep window recesses, the square as the civic room; `celtic` — rubble stone lime-washed, thatch or slate on low hipped roofs with rounded ends, small windows, tower houses, longhouse crofts with byres, little ornament but carved stone; `norse` — log and stave timber tarred or stained red and ochre, steep shingle or turf roofs, longhouse axes, raised storehouses on posts, stave-church tiers with ridge beasts on worship, carved portals; `slavic` — log houses with steep shingle roofs, carved window surrounds and gable boards, whitewashed masonry where stone, tented and onion roofs on worship, timber-walled enclosures, wooden pavements; `arabic` — inward courtyard houses behind blank street walls, flat roofs with parapets and rooftop life, lattice screens, pointed and horseshoe arches, domes and minarets on worship, vaulted covered markets, whitewash and tilework, narrow shaded lanes; `east_asian` — post-and-beam timber on raised plinths, hip-and-gable tile roofs with deep upturned eaves, walled courtyard compounds with gates, axial symmetry, lattice screens, grey tile over white plaster and red columns, tiered towers; `south_asian` — stone and brick under lime plaster, flat roofs with rooftop pavilions, overhanging carved balconies and brackets, courtyard mansions, cusped arches, bright colour, verandas, stepped temple towers, tanks and stepwells; `mesoamerican` — stone platforms and stepped pyramids, red and white stucco, flat-roofed masonry for the elite and thatched pole-and-wattle for the rest, houses on low platforms around patios, plazas and raised causeways, relief glyph ornament; `steppe` — round felt tents as the dwelling, camps about a great tent, few permanent buildings (a shrine, a caravanserai, a fortified post), palisades, and in the oasis town mud-brick with flat roofs, domed tombs and courtyard caravanserais, felt and knot motifs; `mixed` — the dominant profile with a minority quarter or two in their own, and the market in the dominant's form. (4b) THE LAYOUT SKETCHES (the session, vetoable; the cells of (2) (g)): `germanic` — narrow deep plots in gable-fronted rows, irregular streets widening into a market street or square, the church and town hall on it; `latin` — the trace of a grid under organic infill, tower-houses, arcaded streets, the piazza with the civic hall and cathedral; `greek` — the agora, a grid where the ground is flat and organic terraces where it is not, courtyard houses, external stairs; `celtic` — dispersed enclosures and crofts, a loose cluster at a crossing or a church, few straight lines; `norse` — farmsteads of longhouse axes, few streets, a thing-place and a quay; `slavic` — wide street villages and street towns, a fortified core with the open town below it, wooden paving, greens; `arabic` — a dense organic fabric of cul-de-sacs and courtyard blocks behind blank street walls, the covered market and great mosque at the centre, baths and fountains, few straight streets; `east_asian` — a grid of walled wards on an axis, courtyard compounds down lanes, the market in its own ward, the civic precinct on the axis; `south_asian` — dense organic trade quarters, courtyard mansions, the temple and its tank at the heart, bazaars along the arteries, stepwells; `mesoamerican` — a ceremonial core of plazas and platforms on raised causeways, house clusters on low platforms around patios, a dispersed residential field; `steppe` — rings of tents about the great tent, seasonal and demountable; the oasis town organic with a citadel and caravanserais; `mixed` — the founders' layout with a later quarter or two in the newcomers' street form on new ground. (5) THE BARS: T-V2 and T-V11 gain the cells-only bar and the envelope bars of (3); T-V2-25 stands (materials never massing) and (0) adds its converse (a culture never selects a material); V06 gains the integration bars of (1) — a founded building's culture distributed as the epoch's ratio within tolerance, new ground laid with the right culture's cells, no built street retrofitted, a renovation changing facade, ornament and interior and never massing or plot, quarters and signal buildings only above their thresholds; the ornament instances hold the §2.9 document-size test (derived, unstored); T-V12's calibration adds, per culture, the roof pitch band, the opening rhythm, the arcade and jetty shares, the cul-de-sac share, the plot aspect and the block form shares against reference plans of the vernacular (clean-room: measurements, never drawings). (6) THE URBAN GRAMMARS (THE OWNER, 2026-10-31, recorded 02:11Z, J664: 'we've pretty much nailed the European model and I don't think we have to worry much about the rural area, but for urban Mesoamerica, for example, or others, when we put down the central pieces of the city it's a lot of terraces and pyramidal structures, with those central buildings or institutions at the top and then everything else kind of flows down from there'; the session's proposal, vetoable): the European pipeline S3–S10 is ONE URBAN GRAMMAR — `street` (streets bound blocks; the market square at the convergence; the built and calibrated one, the reference) — and the layout row of (2) (g) names the culture's grammar among four: `street`; `grid` (Teotihuacan, Tenochtitlan, the East Asian walled wards, the Roman trace: S4's grid grammar with an axis, S6's walled wards, the market in its ward — cells, no new machinery; canals through S1's water operator); `field` (the Maya lowland city: a monumental PRECINCT at the core, CAUSEWAYS radiating to secondary plaza groups, and a RESIDENTIAL FIELD of patio groups on platforms in open ground with infields between — no continuous street net, no block cutting); `camp` (the steppe: rings about the great tent, seasonal). THE FIELD GRAMMAR IS MOSTLY WHAT WE HAVE: V07's dispersed and polyfocal forms raised to city scale — S3's polycentric nuclei become the secondary plaza groups, S5 grows polyfocally about them, S6's districts are the groups' barrios, S8 and S10 run in a PLACEMENT MODE (platform footprints placed along causeways and about patios as V07 places farmsteads at track ends, the plot law in placement form, the infields the §4.5 fields) instead of cutting blocks; what is NEW is three things: (a) THE PRECINCT — S3's castle claim (compactness, the overlap rules, F-CASTLE's ladder) generalised to a `precinct` claim composed by a town-scale grammar: an axis, a sequence of plazas, platform footprints at the plaza edges, the head pyramid at the axis's end, palaces and a ballcourt along the sides, each platform a `stepped` composition (V02's family, V11 §3.24.3's rows, V04's `layTiers`), the precinct the town's highest ground and its tallest tiers; (b) THE TERRACE OPERATOR — a terrain write at S3 that levels the precinct and terraces the slopes it stands on (cut and fill within a max per cell), with its own failure class F-TERRACE (level → stepped terraces → the natural slope with V02's stepped wings and skirts), the one place this grammar writes terrain, bounded like the river carve; (c) THE CAUSEWAYS — S4's arterials in a `causeway` class: straight, raised (§2.7's raised-way laws, V04's vaults under raised ways, V06's `raisedWays` step), from the precinct's plaza edges to the groups and the water, under F-ROUTE's ladder. THE GRADIENT ('everything flows down from there'): under the `field` and `grid` grammars the prosperity field's drivers (M2b) weight centrality and elevation heavily, and the band sets the platform's tiers and the compound's size, so height, formality and density step down from the precinct outward and downhill — the hierarchy is read off the field, never drawn by hand. THE KERNEL IS UNTOUCHED: a platform is a face, a causeway a chain, a terrace a terrain field, a plaza an open face — every grammar commits through the same transactions and ladders (§5). TWO MESOAMERICAN SUB-MODELS: the lowland `field` city and the highland `grid` city (Teotihuacan's avenue, Tenochtitlan's canals), both `mesoamerican`, chosen by the era and region row or the dossier. WHERE AND WHEN: V01's template fabric (M5) is the natural home — a grammar is a template at town scale — after parity (M2b) on the `street` grammar, which stays first and the reference; the Maya fixture calibrated against public-domain archaeological site plans (measurements, never drawings; clean-room); the rural forms stand as V07 has them (the owner: no worry there). (7) THE THREE LAYERS AFTER SITING — the owner's compromise (2026-10-31, recorded 02:14Z, J666: 'we should have the appropriate key things for all of the other examples … I still think the initial way of choosing where to place everything is correct. It's just how we formulate the layout of things after placing those central things, and then the designation of the streets'), the session's comprehensive reading (vetoable): SITING IS UNIVERSAL — S1–S3 (terrain, water, demand, the site, the centre, the nuclei, the claims) run one algorithm for every culture, with the culture allowed only WEIGHTS in S3's candidate tests (a cell set: the water preference — a river bank, a harbour, a cenote or a tank; the ground preference — a hilltop citadel or a flat plain; the AXIS ORIENTATION — a south-facing precinct, a shore-parallel spine) and never a different law; after the central pieces are placed, three layers carry the culture, in this order: LAYER 1, THE CORE COMPOSITION — a town-scale template per culture for how the central pieces compose about their open space (the market square with church and hall; the piazza with cathedral and civic hall on a grid trace; the agora; the fortified core above the open town; the great mosque with the covered market, the baths and the citadel at the edge; the axial government precinct with its towers; the temple and its tank; the precinct of plazas and platforms; the great tent), generalising S3's castle claim and S7's civic-core composition into a `coreTemplate` row, the biggest single lever of legibility; LAYER 2, THE FILL GRAMMAR — one of (6)'s four (`street`, `grid`, `field`, `camp`), fewer than the cultures, each culture naming one with its own cells (S5's growth mode, S6's district form, S8's cut form, S10's plot aspect), so the grammars are shared machinery and the cultures differ in values; LAYER 3, THE WAY DESIGNATION — a culture-keyed WAY-CLASS TABLE (`WayClassRow`s per culture) in place of today's one fixed list (V07's `through`, `primary`, `secondary`, `lane`, `alley`, `cul_de_sac`, `track`, `path`): per class its width band, straightness, section (level, raised causeway, sunken, covered gallery, canal), its dead-end admissibility, its gate rule and the hierarchy's depth (the public thoroughfare → the quarter street → the gated cul-de-sac of the Islamic city; the avenue → the ward gate → the lane of the Chinese city; the causeway → the path of the Maya city; the shore street → the plank lane of the Norse town), read by S4's router, S5's growth and S9's corridor union as data — the kernel's corridor union already takes widths as data, so the generalisation is a table, not an operation. THE KEY PIECES PER CULTURE (the session's first reading; the rows to be calibrated): `germanic` — market square or market street with church and hall / street / arterial-secondary-lane-alley within wall rings, gates, suburbs at the gates; `latin` — piazza on a grid trace with cathedral and hall / street with a grid remnant / arcaded streets, the cardo-decumanus trace; `greek` — agora / grid on the flat, terraces on the hill / orthogonal streets and stepped lanes; `celtic` — church or crossing / street (the planted town: one market street with burgage plots; the native pattern dispersed) / a single spine; `norse` — thing-place and quay / street along the shore / a shore street and plank lanes; `slavic` — fortified core above the open town / street / wide streets, wooden paving, the core's gates; `arabic` — great mosque, covered market, baths, citadel at the edge / street in courtyard-block form / thoroughfare → quarter street → gated cul-de-sac, covered galleries; `east_asian` — axial government precinct with drum and bell towers / grid of walled wards / avenues, ward gates, lanes, canals where the water is; `south_asian` — temple and tank, or fort, palace and congregational mosque / street in dense trade quarters / bazaar arteries, gated dead-end quarters, stepwells and ghats; `mesoamerican` — precinct of plazas and platforms / field (lowland) or grid (highland) / causeways and paths, or avenues and canals; `steppe` — the great tent or a shrine / camp, and in the oasis town the citadel with caravanserais and bazaar / paths, then organic lanes; `mixed` — the founders'. ORDER OF COST: `street` is built; `grid` and `camp` are cells and a ring rule; `field` needs (6)'s three new pieces; the way-class table and the core templates are two bounded generalisations that every grammar then shares. REJECTED: a pipeline per culture (twelve pipelines cannot be kept honest); culture-specific kernel operations; a different siting law per culture (weights only). (8) VARIATION WITHIN A CULTURE (THE OWNER, 2026-10-31, recorded 02:36Z, J669: 'make sure there's enough variation … I don't want every single city looking the same, nor every single Greek or Celtic or Mesoamerican … random numbers and bands, and weighted rolls rather than identical obvious features … they should recognise that this is Aztec, but they should not recognise the same Aztec temple or layout in every city'): written as §4.14 (15) — every culture cell a BAND drawn once per town from the town's stream, every core template a weighted set of variants, every signal building a weighted pool with composition bands, the exterior weights drawn per building inside the town's draw, quarter-level draws where the grammar allows; the recognition bar, the within-culture and within-town dispersion floors, the envelope as the ceiling; V11 §3.25b's `Band` type and variants, V02 §3.17b's sentence. KEPT SMALL (the session's reading, vetoable): one exterior row and one layout row per culture, the layout row nothing but values for cells the stages already declare; the profile multiplies and selects within the class rows, never adds a law; the signal buildings through the one specialisation table that exists; the mix only through V06's three channels of (1), the thresholds two cells; rejected: a mix drawn at creation (J660's reading, REVERSED by J662 — a town is born homogeneous), culture-keyed LAWS (the laws stay; the cells move), new massing families per culture (the class rows' family sets suffice; tiers already exist where a culture needs them), materials by culture (J662: the resources decide), materials changing massing (the owner's 2026-09-26 rule), a retrofit of built streets. | the owner, 2026-10-31 (J660; the focus, the homogeneous birth and the mix by time, J662; the urban grammars, J664; the three layers after siting, J666); the session's sketches (vetoable); V08 §2.6's `cultures` row, V11 §3.24.3, V11 §3.19, DR43, V06's epoch rows, V07's `cultureForms` | V02's owner at the composition rows' design round with V11's row owner; V06's owner for the integration channels; V08's dossier owner for the epoch ratios (the owner's host side); V03's owner for the glyph rows (with Q121) | DESIGN INTEGRATED (J668): ARCHITECTURE §4.14 (the culture layer, with the twelve-culture table), the F-TERRACE row of §5.2, the culture pointers of §4.2–§4.7, the §16 clauses of M5, M6, M6-E, M6-P and M-CAL; V11 §3.25b (the five row types and their compile bars), V02 §3.17b, V06 §3.19b (+ W95), V08 §2.6 `cultureShares` (+ W102), V07 §2.4, V01 §3.4, V12 §3.11 (three rows); V03's glyph rows ride Q121 (14)–(15) at its design round — OPEN for the rows' VALUES at M5 / M6a / M6-P / M6-E / M-CAL (J660, J662, J664, J666, J668, J669) |
